Equation <a href="#rhyb:row-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:row-cost">[rhyb:row-cost]</a> then gives <a href="#rhyb:row-log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:row-log-bound">[rhyb:row-log-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

The independent-kernel estimate bounds the actual projected density at the scale $A$. To compare this scale to a fresh conditional entropy, we use two fresh rows after the $r$ common rows. The second of the two is the next slope beyond the one already present in the hybrid identity.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A reciprocal-distance tilt and two fresh rows

<a id="stmt-rhyb-tilted-coupling"></a>
<div id="rhyb:tilted-coupling" class="lemma">

<a id="rhyb:next-slope"></a>
**Lemma 20** (The next fresh entropy slope). *For the posterior and scale in <a href="#rhyb:reciprocal-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:reciprocal-scale">[rhyb:reciprocal-scale]</a>, $$\label{rhyb:next-slope}
 a_{r+2}^{(i)}\le-\mathbb E\log A(S,L,F_r)+C.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rhyb-tilted-coupling"><summary>Proof of stmt-rhyb-tilted-coupling</summary>

*Proof.* Write $C_*=(L,F_r)$. Conditional on $C_*$, the signal has law $\mu$. For an integer $N\ge1$, define $$w_N(s,t)=\min\{N,\|t-s\|^{-1}\},\qquad
 A_N(s,C_*)=\int w_N(s,t)\,\mu(dt),$$ where $w_N(s,s)=N$. Given $S=s,C_*$, draw an auxiliary point $S^*$ from the probability kernel $$\nu_s^N(dt)=\frac{w_N(s,t)}{A_N(s,C_*)}\,\mu(dt).$$ Distances on the unit sphere are at most two, so $1/2\le A_N\le N$ and $d\nu_s^N/d\mu\le2N$. The marginal law of $S^*$ need not equal $\mu$.

<a id="rhyb:tilt-information"></a>
Compare the joint conditional law of $(S,S^*)$ with $\mu\otimes\mu$. Its divergence is $$\mathbb E\log\frac{w_N(S,S^*)}{A_N(S,C_*)}.$$ The relative-entropy chain rule splits this into $I(S;S^*\mid C_*)$ plus the divergence of the actual $S^*$ marginal from the comparator $\mu$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}
 I(S;S^*\mid C_*)
 &\le\mathbb E\log\frac{w_N(S,S^*)}{A_N(S,C_*)}\notag\\
 &\le-\mathbb E\log\|S-S^*\|
       -\mathbb E\log A_N(S,C_*).\label{rhyb:tilt-information}
\end{aligned}$$ The comparator divergence is finite, at most $\log(2N)$. The points differ almost surely because $\mu$ is nonatomic. Their log distance is absolutely integrable: its positive part is at most $\log2$; under $\mu\otimes\mu$ its negative part is bounded by an integrable multiple of $\|S-S^*\|^{-1}$ by <a href="#rhyb:scale-integrability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:scale-integrability">[rhyb:scale-integrability]</a>, and the tilted density is at most $2N$. Thus both inequalities in <a href="#rhyb:tilt-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:tilt-information">[rhyb:tilt-information]</a> are between well-defined finite quantities.

<a id="rhyb:remove-coupling"></a>
After drawing $S^*$, generate independent fresh Gaussian rows $x_1,x_2$ and labels $b_1=x_1S$, $b_2=x_2S$. Before $S^*$ is added to the conditioning, Lemma <a href="#rhyb:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:residual">16</a> gives a joint conditional density with an integrable logarithm for these labels, since $i+r+2=k+1\le3d/4$. Fresh-row independence and data processing give $$\begin{aligned}
 I(b_2;S^*\mid b_1,x_1,x_2,C_*)
 &\le I((b_1,b_2);S^*\mid x_1,x_2,C_*)\notag\\
 &\le I(S;S^*\mid C_*).\label{rhyb:remove-coupling}
\end{aligned}$$ This is finite. The log-density-ratio argument from Lemma <a href="#rhyb:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:residual">16</a> consequently gives integrable conditional log densities after $S^*$ is added, both for the joint labels and for the first label. Their entropy chain rule justifies the conditional entropy of the second label used next.

Given $b_1,x_1,x_2,C_*,S^*$, compare the density of $b_2$ with the Cauchy density centered at $x_2S^*$ and with scale $|b_1-x_1S^*|$. The scale is positive almost surely. With $\Delta=S-S^*$, the expected negative log of this reference density at $b_2$ is <a id="rhyb:cauchy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\log\pi+\mathbb E\log|x_1\Delta|
 +\mathbb E\log\left(1+\frac{(x_2\Delta)^2}{(x_1\Delta)^2}\right)
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{35mm}=\mathbb E\log\|\Delta\|+C_1,\label{rhyb:cauchy}
\end{aligned}$$ where $C_1$ is absolute. Conditional on nonzero $\Delta$, the two projections divided by $\|\Delta\|$ are independent standard normals $G_1,G_2$. The first logarithm is integrable because $\mathbb E|\log|G_1||<\infty$. For the ratio term, $$\log\bigl(1+(G_2/G_1)^2\bigr)
 =\log(G_1^2+G_2^2)-\log G_1^2,$$ and both logarithms on the right are integrable by the one- and two-dimensional chi-squared densities. The cross-entropy inequality therefore gives $$h(b_2\mid b_1,x_1,x_2,C_*,S^*)
 \le\mathbb E\log\|\Delta\|+C_1.$$

Removing $S^*$ from the conditioning in this entropy bound and using <a href="#rhyb:tilt-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:tilt-information">[rhyb:tilt-information]</a>–<a href="#rhyb:remove-coupling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:remove-coupling">[rhyb:remove-coupling]</a> cancels $\mathbb E\log\|\Delta\|$. We obtain $$h(b_2\mid b_1,x_1,x_2,C_*)\le-\mathbb E\log A_N(S,C_*)+C_1.$$ The left side is exactly $a_{r+2}^{(i)}$ and is independent of $N$. Finally $A_N\uparrow A$ and $\log A_N\ge-\log2$. Monotone convergence, with the integrability of $\log A$ already proved, yields <a href="#rhyb:next-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:next-slope">[rhyb:next-slope]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Averaging over the number of side measurements

The row comparison and the two-row coupling now leave only a difference of consecutive fresh entropy slopes. Averaging over consecutive values of $k$ makes this difference telescope.

<details id="proof-stmt-rhyb-tilted-coupling-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-rhyb-tilted-coupling</summary>

<a id="rhyb:slope-difference"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#rhyb:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:block">15</a>.* Equations <a href="#rhyb:cross-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:cross-entropy">[rhyb:cross-entropy]</a> and <a href="#rhyb:row-log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:row-log-bound">[rhyb:row-log-bound]</a> lower-bound the bracket in <a href="#rhyb:hybrid-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:hybrid-step">[rhyb:hybrid-step]</a> by $-\mathbb E\log A-C-K_i/q$. Equation <a href="#rhyb:next-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:next-slope">[rhyb:next-slope]</a> then gives $$h(xS\mid x,L,F_r)+I(S;x\mid L,F_r)
 \ge a_{r+2}^{(i)}-C-K_i/q.$$ Since $r=k-i-1$, the hybrid identity becomes $$\label{rhyb:slope-difference}
 G_i(k)-G_{i+1}(k)
 \le a_{k-i}^{(i)}-a_{k-i+1}^{(i)}+C+K_i/q.$$

For fixed $i$, the fresh observations are exchangeable conditional on $L_i$. Relabel two consecutive fresh observations and then condition on the additional earlier one. Conditional entropy can only decrease, so the successive slopes $a_j^{(i)}$ are nonincreasing in their finite range. Also $$a_j^{(i)}\le\tfrac12\log(2\pi e)$$ by comparison with the marginal standard normal label.

<a id="rhyb:slope-lower"></a>
For $i+j\le k_1+1$, a useful lower bound is $$\label{rhyb:slope-lower}
 a_j^{(i)}\ge-C-B/h_0.$$ To prove it, use monotonicity to bound $a_j^{(i)}$ below by the average of the next $h_0$ slopes. Their sum is the joint entropy of the next $h_0$ fresh labels given their rows, $D'_j$, and $L_i$. Removing $V$ from that conditioning increases the entropy by at most $H(V)\le B$. Without $V$, the actual prefix and all fresh rows have the ordinary independent-row law. Each successive baseline label entropy is at least $-C$ by Lemma <a href="#rhyb:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:residual">16</a>, since the total number of rows is at most $$i+j+h_0\le k_1+1+h_0\le3d/4$$ for sufficiently large $d$. The sum is therefore at least $-Ch_0-B$, proving <a href="#rhyb:slope-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:slope-lower">[rhyb:slope-lower]</a>.

Average <a href="#rhyb:slope-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:slope-difference">[rhyb:slope-difference]</a> over $k_0\le k\le k_1$. For each $i$, its slope term is exactly $$\frac1{N_k}\sum_{k=k_0}^{k_1}
 \bigl(a_{k-i}^{(i)}-a_{k-i+1}^{(i)}\bigr)
 =\frac{a_{k_0-i}^{(i)}-a_{k_1-i+1}^{(i)}}{N_k}
 \le\frac{C+B/h_0}{N_k}.$$ Now sum over $i=0,\ldots,m-1$, use $\sum_iK_i\le B$ from <a href="#rhyb:row-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:row-budget">[rhyb:row-budget]</a>, and apply <a href="#rhyb:block-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:block-reduction">[rhyb:block-reduction]</a>. This gives <a href="#rhyb:block-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:block-cost">[rhyb:block-cost]</a>. Finally $N_k,h_0,q\ge cd$ and $m\le d/8$, so $B\le d^2$ makes the displayed block cost at most $C'd$. ◻

</details>

Subsection <a href="#subsec:r-hybrid-application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:r-hybrid-application">8.5</a> combines this block estimate with the stopping clock and the terminal residual-sphere bound.

<a id="sec:r-fiber"></a>
### A finite Gaussian-fiber comparison

We now bound the information retained after a block of exact Gaussian measurements by comparing two ways to average the state likelihood along a measurement fiber. The comparison measure is finite but need not have mass one. Its marginal is an inverse-distance kernel involving the rows actually used by the transition. A common local-mass quantity then connects that marginal to a projection independent of the new state.

<a id="rfib:parameters"></a>
We retain the measure and entropy conventions of Section <a href="#sec:r-experiments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:r-experiments">2</a>. Constants denoted by $K$ are positive and absolute and may increase. The local row counts are $$\label{rfib:parameters}
 n=\lfloor d/10\rfloor,\qquad
 k=\lfloor d/3\rfloor,\qquad
 \ell=\lfloor d/10\rfloor.$$ Here $n$ is the number of rows read in one block, $k$ is the number of rows in an auxiliary projection, and $\ell$ is an integer moment order. We take $d$ sufficiently large that $\ell\ge2$ and $k-\ell-n\ge d/10$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Projection fibers and conditional information

<a id="rfib:cap"></a>
We first record the geometric facts used both in the block estimate and at the terminal state. If $\sigma_m$ is uniform probability on $S^{m-1}$, then for $m\ge2$, $z\in S^{m-1}$, and $0<r\le1/2$, $$\label{rfib:cap}
 \sigma_m(B(z,r))\le r^{m-1}.$$ This is the centered-ball estimate of Lemma <a href="#lem:r-caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-caps">3</a> in dimension $m$.

<a id="rfib:projection-density"></a>
<a id="rfib:label-density"></a>
For a full-row-rank $b\times d$ matrix $C$, where $1\le b\le d-2$, put $$P_C=C^{\mathsf T}(CC^{\mathsf T})^{-1}C,\qquad
 x_C(z)=C^{\mathsf T}(CC^{\mathsf T})^{-1}z .$$ Write $S(H)=\{u\in H:\|u\|=1\}$ for the unit sphere of a subspace $H$. The conditional law of $S\sim\sigma$ given $CS=z$, denoted by $\sigma_{C,z}$, fixes $P_CS=x_C(z)$ and is uniform on the remaining sphere $$x_C(z)+\sqrt{1-\|x_C(z)\|^2}\,
       S(\ker C),\qquad \|x_C(z)\|<1.$$ This formula specifies a Borel probability kernel on the indicated set. For example, the uniform direction in $\ker C$ can be realized as $(I-P_C)Z/\|(I-P_C)Z\|$ with $Z$ a standard Gaussian vector; the map is Borel away from a null set. Set the kernel to a fixed point mass off the full-rank interior set. To verify the formula, write a uniform sphere point as a standard Gaussian vector divided by its length. The squared lengths in the row space and its orthogonal complement are independent chi-squared variables with $b$ and $d-b$ degrees of freedom before division. The first divided by their sum has the beta distribution with parameters $b/2$ and $(d-b)/2$, and the two directions are independent and uniform. Consequently the row-space projection, in orthonormal coordinates, has density $$\label{rfib:projection-density}
 c_{d,b}(1-\|x\|^2)^{(d-b-2)/2}\quad(\|x\|<1),
 \qquad
 c_{d,b}=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}
               {\pi^{b/2}\Gamma((d-b)/2)} .$$ The density is zero outside the unit ball. In particular, the conditional statement holds for almost every observation. Since $\|x_C(z)\|^2=z^{\mathsf T}(CC^{\mathsf T})^{-1}z$, the Lebesgue density $p_C$ of $CS$ is $$\label{rfib:label-density}
 p_C(z)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle
 \frac{c_{d,b}}{\det(CC^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}}
 \bigl(1-\|x_C(z)\|^2\bigr)^{(d-b-2)/2},
       &\|x_C(z)\|<1,\\
 0,    &\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Boundary values do not affect any of the integrals below.

<a id="rfib:projection-ball"></a>
For $b=k$, the exponent in <a href="#rfib:projection-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:projection-density">[rfib:projection-density]</a> is nonnegative. Integrating its normalization over the ball of radius $1/2$ shows that its supremum times the volume of the unit $k$-ball is at most $2^k(4/3)^{d/2}$. Thus, for every ball $D$ of radius $r$ in the row space, $$\label{rfib:projection-ball}
 \Pr\{P_CS\in D\}\le 2^k(4/3)^{d/2}r^k\le e^{Kd}r^k.$$ The estimate is for the unweighted uniform measure $\sigma$.

Let $V$ take values in a finite set $\mathcal V$ and be jointly distributed with $S$. Choose Borel versions of the state likelihoods $$f_v(s)=\Pr\{V=v\mid S=s\},\qquad
 \bar f_v(C,z)=\int f_v(t)\,d\sigma_{C,z}(t).$$ Modify the first family on a Borel $\sigma$-null set, if needed, so that $f_v\ge0$ and $\sum_v f_v=1$ everywhere. These are likelihoods, not probability densities normalized separately for each state. When $C$ has $k$ independent standard Gaussian rows and is independent of $(S,V)$, disintegration identifies $\bar f_v(C,z)$ with $\Pr\{V=v\mid C,CS=z\}$. The conditional relative-entropy chain rule therefore gives <a id="rfib:potential"></a>$$\label{rfib:potential}
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi(V)&:=I(S;V\mid C,CS)
 =\mathbb E\log\frac{f_V(S)}{\bar f_V(C,CS)}\\
 &=H(V\mid C,CS)-H(V\mid S).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $H$ denotes Shannon entropy in nats. For the standard-Borel form of this chain rule, see <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Austin2020](#ref-Austin2020), Section 3.3]. Nonnegativity of conditional relative entropy and the displayed difference give $0\le\Phi(V)\le\log|\mathcal V|$. Terms on zero-probability state or fiber events are omitted.

<a id="stmt-rfib-block"></a>
<div id="rfib:block" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Information through a Gaussian block). *Let $S\sim\sigma$, let $V$ be finite-valued, and let $A$ have $n$ independent standard Gaussian rows, independently of $(S,V)$. Suppose $W$ takes at most $N$ values, where $N\ge1$ is an integer, and is obtained from $(V,A,AS)$ by a Borel transition kernel. If $\log N\le d^2$, then for the dimensions in <a href="#rfib:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:parameters">[rfib:parameters]</a> and all sufficiently large $d$, <a id="rfib:drift"></a>$$\label{rfib:drift}
 \Phi(W)\le\Phi(V)+Kd .$$ The auxiliary matrix in each occurrence of $\Phi$ is independent of the pair consisting of the signal and the state in that occurrence.*

</div>

The proof first compares the real law to a finite measure. We next identify the latter’s signal/state marginal by a two-point Borel measure identity. Finally, two estimates with different geometric proofs compare that marginal and the fresh-projection likelihood to one local mass.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The finite measure and its exact marginal

<a id="rfib:routing"></a>
Write the transition probabilities as $h_{vw}(A,AS)$. Define $$\label{rfib:routing}
 g_w(A,t)=\sum_v f_v(t)h_{vw}(A,At),\qquad
 f_w(t)=\mathbb E_A g_w(A,t).$$ Thus $0\le g_w\le1$, $0\le f_w\le1$, and the sums over $w$ of each of these functions are one. The function $f_w$ is precisely the new state’s likelihood given $S=t$. In particular neither $g_w$ nor $f_w$ is divided by the marginal probability of state $w$.

<a id="rfib:PQ"></a>
Augment $A$ by $k-n$ independent Gaussian rows and call the result $C$, with $A$ as its first $n$ rows. Relative to the product measure $\mathfrak m$ of the Gaussian law of $C$, $\sigma(ds)$, and counting measure on the two state sets, the real law $P$ and the comparison measure $Q$ on $(C,S,V,W)$ have densities $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{dP}{d\mathfrak m}(C,s,v,w)
   &=f_v(s)h_{vw}(A,As),\notag\\
 \frac{dQ}{d\mathfrak m}(C,s,v,w)
   &=p_C(Cs)\bar f_v(C,Cs)h_{vw}(A,As).
   \label{rfib:PQ}
\end{aligned}$$ The factor $p_C(Cs)$ turns the conditional fiber average $\bar f_v(C,Cs)$ into the density at $Cs$ of the projection of $f_v\,d\sigma$. This unnormalized equal-label measure will yield the inverse-distance kernel in Lemma <a href="#rfib:two-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:two-point">22</a>. The routing weights in $Q$ remain the actual weights $h_{vw}(A,As)$. We have $P\ll Q$: $p_C(Cs)>0$ almost surely, and a fiber on which $\bar f_v(C,Cs)=0$ has $f_v=0$ almost everywhere on that fiber.

The comparison measure has strictly positive finite mass. Indeed, summing the routing probabilities and then the old-state likelihoods gives $$m_Q:=Q(\Omega)
 =\mathbb E_C\int p_C(Cs)\,d\sigma(s)
 =\mathbb E_C\int p_C(z)^2\,dz
 \le\mathbb E_C\sup_z p_C(z)<\infty.$$ The last expectation is finite by <a href="#rfib:label-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:label-density">[rfib:label-density]</a>. Gram–Schmidt on the Gaussian rows expresses $\det(CC^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}$ as a product of successive perpendicular lengths with conditional chi laws of degrees $d,d-1,\ldots,d-k+1$. A chi variable of degree $\nu>1$ has finite inverse first moment $2^{-1/2}\Gamma((\nu-1)/2)/\Gamma(\nu/2)$, and all these degrees are greater than one. Strict positivity follows from the positivity of $p_C$ on the interior of its support.

<a id="rfib:finite-entropy"></a>
Use the signed finite-reference entropy $D_{\mathrm{fin}}(P\|Q)=\mathbb E_P\log(dP/dQ)$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:r-finite-reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-finite-reference">4</a>. Cancellation in <a href="#rfib:PQ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:PQ">[rfib:PQ]</a>, together with the independence of $C$ from $(S,V)$, yields $$\label{rfib:finite-entropy}
 D_{\mathrm{fin}}(P\|Q)=\Phi(V)+\mathbb E[-\log p_C(CS)]
 \le\Phi(V)+\frac{k}{2}\log(2\pi e).$$ Here $\log p_C(CS)$ is integrable: the logarithm of the determinant has finite log moments by the same chi factorization, and the residual factor in <a href="#rfib:label-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:label-density">[rfib:label-density]</a> has finite log moments by the beta law. For the inequality, let $\varphi_k$ be the standard Gaussian density. Nonnegativity of $D(p_C\,dz\|\varphi_k\,dz)$, integrated over $C$, gives $\mathbb E[-\log p_C(CS)]\le\mathbb E[-\log\varphi_k(CS)]$. Unconditionally $CS$ is standard Gaussian, because $S$ has unit length and is independent of the Gaussian rows. This assertion is not a conditional Gaussian assertion given $C$ or the state.

<a id="rfib:finite-dp"></a>
It is necessary to normalize $Q$ before invoking probability data processing. Set $Q_0=Q/m_Q$, and let $\pi$ be any measurable map. Both $Q$ and its pushforward have mass $m_Q$, so $$\begin{aligned}
 D_{\mathrm{fin}}(P\|Q)&=D(P\|Q_0)-\log m_Q, \notag\\
 D_{\mathrm{fin}}(\pi_\#P\|\pi_\#Q)
   &=D(\pi_\#P\|\pi_\#Q_0)-\log m_Q \notag\\
   &\le D(P\|Q_0)-\log m_Q
     =D_{\mathrm{fin}}(P\|Q).                         \label{rfib:finite-dp}
\end{aligned}$$ No nonnegativity of $D_{\mathrm{fin}}(P\|Q)$ is used.

<a id="rfib:marginal-entropy"></a>
Let $q_w(s)$ be the density of the $(S,W)$ marginal of $Q$ relative to $\sigma$ and counting measure. The corresponding real density is $f_w(s)$. Equations <a href="#rfib:finite-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:finite-entropy">[rfib:finite-entropy]</a> and <a href="#rfib:finite-dp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:finite-dp">[rfib:finite-dp]</a> imply $$\label{rfib:marginal-entropy}
 \mathbb E\log\frac{f_W(S)}{q_W(S)}
 \le\Phi(V)+Kd ,$$ where this expectation, and all state expectations below, are under the real law. The densities $q_w$ need not sum to a probability density.

We next calculate this marginal in a form suitable for geometric estimates. Let $\gamma_b$ denote the law of $b$ unrestricted standard Gaussian rows. For a unit vector $u$, let $\gamma_{b,\perp u}$ be the law of $b$ independent rows with Gaussian covariance $I-uu^{\mathsf T}$, supported on $u^\perp$.

<a id="stmt-rfib-two-point"></a>
<div id="rfib:two-point" class="lemma">

<a id="rfib:two-point-id"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Two-point Gaussian fiber measure). *Assume $1\le k<d-1$. For every bounded Borel function $H(C,s,t)$ on $\mathbb R^{k\times d}\times S^{d-1}\times S^{d-1}$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int \gamma_k(dC)\int\sigma(ds)\,p_C(Cs)
       \int\sigma_{C,Cs}(dt)\,H(C,s,t) \notag\\
 &\quad=(2\pi)^{-k/2}\int\sigma(ds)\int\sigma(dt)\,
       \|s-t\|^{-k}
       \int\gamma_{k,\perp\widehat{s-t}}(dC)\,H(C,s,t),
                                                        \label{rfib:two-point-id}
\end{aligned}$$ where $\widehat{s-t}=(s-t)/\|s-t\|$ off the diagonal. The diagonal has product measure zero, and its assigned value is immaterial. Both sides define finite Borel measures.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rfib-two-point"><summary>Proof of stmt-rfib-two-point</summary>

*Proof.* For $\delta>0$, let $\kappa_\delta(z)=(2\pi\delta^2)^{-k/2}
 e^{-\|z\|^2/(2\delta^2)}$. First let $H$ be bounded and continuous, and integrate $$H(C,s,t)\kappa_\delta(C(t-s))
 \quad\text{against}\quad
 \gamma_k(dC)\sigma(ds)\sigma(dt).$$ Fix full-rank $C$ and $s$ with $\|P_Cs\|<1$. Disintegration in $z=Ct$ makes the integral in $t$ $$\int\kappa_\delta(z-Cs)\,p_C(z)
       \left(\int H(C,s,t)\,d\sigma_{C,z}(t)\right)dz.$$ The function multiplied by $\kappa_\delta$ is continuous at $Cs$. Indeed the displayed fiber parametrization is continuous at interior observations, and the test is bounded and continuous. It is bounded in absolute value by $\|H\|_\infty\sup_zp_C(z)$. The Gaussian approximate identity therefore converges to the left integrand in <a href="#rfib:two-point-id" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:two-point-id">[rfib:two-point-id]</a>; the preceding inverse determinant estimate makes this bound integrable in $C$.

Alternatively fix $s\ne t$, and integrate first in $C$. Writing $v=t-s$, each row integral has total weight $$(2\pi)^{-1/2}(\|v\|^2+\delta^2)^{-1/2}.$$ After division by that weight, the row is a centered Gaussian with covariance $I-vv^{\mathsf T}/(\|v\|^2+\delta^2)$. As $\delta\downarrow0$ this law converges to the Gaussian law on $v^\perp$. The $k$ rows remain independent, so the limit is the right integrand in <a href="#rfib:two-point-id" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:two-point-id">[rfib:two-point-id]</a>. Its absolute value is dominated by $(2\pi)^{-k/2}\|H\|_\infty\|s-t\|^{-k}$. This is integrable under $\sigma\otimes\sigma$: dyadic annuli about $s$, with <a href="#rfib:cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:cap">[rfib:cap]</a> for the small radii, give a convergent geometric series because $k<d-1$.

The two orders compute the same limit. They prove equality on bounded continuous tests of two finite Borel measures on the indicated Polish space. Uniqueness of finite Borel measures then proves the equality for bounded Borel tests as well. This last step is what permits measurable routing functions in the application. ◻

</details>

<a id="rfib:kernel"></a>
On the fiber $Ct=Cs$, the first $n$ rows give $At=As$. Consequently, using <a href="#rfib:routing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:routing">[rfib:routing]</a> without normalizing its weights, $$q_w(s)=\mathbb E_C\left[
   p_C(Cs)\int g_w(A,t)\,d\sigma_{C,Cs}(t)\right].$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#rfib:two-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:two-point">22</a> to $H(C,s,t)=\psi(s)g_w(A,t)$ for bounded Borel $\psi$. The extra $k-n$ rows do not enter $g_w$, so integrating them out leaves precisely the marginal law of the $n$ actual rows. We obtain, for $\sigma$-almost every $s$, $$\label{rfib:kernel}
 q_w(s)=(2\pi)^{-k/2}\int
   \|s-t\|^{-k}
   \mathbb E_{A\sim\gamma_{n,\perp\widehat{s-t}}}g_w(A,t)
   \,d\sigma(t).$$ This is an equality of marginal densities of the finite measure $Q$; there is no additional normalization on its right-hand side.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Two estimates for a common local mass

<a id="rfib:local-bounds"></a>
<a id="rfib:cutoff"></a>
Put $a_w=\int f_w\,d\sigma=\Pr\{W=w\}$, and omit states with $a_w=0$. Define <a id="rfib:local-mass"></a>$$\label{rfib:local-mass}
 L_{w,r}(s)=r^{-k}\int_{B(s,r)}f_w(t)\,d\sigma(t),
 \qquad L_w(s)=\sup_{r>0}L_{w,r}(s).$$ For each $r$ the integral is Borel in $s$. Rational radii suffice in the supremum: approximate any radius from above and use continuity from above of the finite measure $f_w\sigma$. Thus $L_w$ is Borel. The diameter-two ball gives its lower bound, while <a href="#rfib:cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:cap">[rfib:cap]</a> for $r\le1/2$ and total mass at most one for larger radii give the upper bound $$\label{rfib:local-bounds}
 2^{-k}a_w\le L_w(s)\le2^k .$$ Choose the state-count-dependent cutoff $$\label{rfib:cutoff}
 J=1+\left\lceil\frac{\log_2N}{d-1-k}\right\rceil,\qquad
 r_*=2^{-J}.$$ Then $r_*\le1/2$, $Nr_*^{d-1-k}\le1$, and $J=O(d)$, since $d-1-k\ge d/2$ for large $d$ and $\log N\le d^2$.

<a id="stmt-rfib-first-ratio"></a>
<div id="rfib:first-ratio" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Comparison with an independent projection). *Let $C'$ have $k$ independent standard Gaussian rows and be independent of $(S,W)$. Then <a id="rfib:ratio-fresh"></a>$$\label{rfib:ratio-fresh}
 \mathbb E\frac{L_W(S)}{\bar f_W(C',C'S)}\le e^{Kd}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rfib-first-ratio"><summary>Proof of stmt-rfib-first-ratio</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a full-rank $C'$ and a radius $r\le2$. Choose a finite maximal $r$-separated set $\{z_i\}$ on the sphere, and partition the sphere into Borel cells $E_i\subseteq B(z_i,r)$, breaking ties by their indices. The enlarged balls $B(z_i,2r)$ overlap at most $5^d$ times: balls of radius $r/2$ about their separated centers have disjoint interiors, and all those meeting a given point in an enlarged ball lie inside the ambient ball of radius $5r/2$ about that point.

<a id="rfib:fixed-radius"></a>
On $E_i$, $$L_{w,r}(s)\le r^{-k}\int_{B(z_i,2r)}f_w\,d\sigma.$$ Write $R$ for the row space of $C'$, $p_R$ for the density in <a href="#rfib:projection-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:projection-density">[rfib:projection-density]</a>, and $\sigma_x$ for the corresponding uniform fiber law. Conditioning on $P_{C'}S=x$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{E_i}\frac{f_w(s)}{\bar f_w(C',C's)}\,d\sigma(s)
 &=\int_R p_R(x)
   \frac{\int_{E_i}f_w(s)\,d\sigma_x(s)}
        {\bar f_w(C',C'x)}\,dx\\
 &\le\Pr\{P_{C'}S\in B(P_{C'}z_i,r)\}
 \le e^{Kd}r^k .
\end{aligned}$$ The quotient is defined to be zero on zero-denominator fibers; on all other fibers its numerator is at most its denominator. The numerator vanishes on fibers outside the displayed projected ball. The last probability is under the unweighted prior and is bounded by <a href="#rfib:projection-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:projection-ball">[rfib:projection-ball]</a>. Multiplying the last two estimates and summing the cells, with their $5^d$ overlap, yields $$\label{rfib:fixed-radius}
 \int\frac{f_w(s)L_{w,r}(s)}
           {\bar f_w(C',C's)}\,d\sigma(s)
 \le e^{Kd}a_w .$$

For $r\ge r_*$, one of the grid radii $2,1,1/2,\ldots,r_*$ gives $L_{w,r}\le2^k L_{w,\mathrm{grid}}$; radii greater than two are already bounded by the radius-two expression. For $r<r_*$, <a href="#rfib:cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:cap">[rfib:cap]</a> gives $L_{w,r}\le r^{d-1-k}\le r_*^{d-1-k}$. A second conditioning on the projection gives $\int f_w/\bar f_w(C',C'\cdot)\,d\sigma\le1$. We may therefore sum <a href="#rfib:fixed-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:fixed-radius">[rfib:fixed-radius]</a> over the grid and over states, obtaining $$\sum_w\int\frac{f_wL_w}{\bar f_w(C',C'\cdot)}\,d\sigma
 \le 2^k(J+2)e^{Kd}\sum_w a_w+Nr_*^{d-1-k}
 \le e^{Kd}.$$ The bound is uniform in full-rank $C'$, and averaging proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="rfib:ratio-kernel"></a>
<a id="rfib:nu-ball"></a>
The other comparison is with the actual finite-measure marginal: $$\label{rfib:ratio-kernel}
 \mathbb E\frac{q_W(S)}{L_W(S)}\le e^{Kd}.$$ We prove it now. For each positive-mass state introduce the finite measure $$d\nu_w(s)=\frac{f_w(s)}{L_w(s)}\,d\sigma(s).$$ Equation <a href="#rfib:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:local-bounds">[rfib:local-bounds]</a> gives $\nu_w(S^{d-1})\le2^k$. More locally, for every ambient center $z$ and $r>0$, $$\label{rfib:nu-ball}
 \nu_w(B(z,r))\le(2r)^k .$$ Indeed, if $m=\int_{B(z,r)}f_w\,d\sigma>0$, every $s$ in that ball on the sphere has $L_w(s)\ge(2r)^{-k}m$; integration of $f_w/L_w$ over the ball gives the bound. The case $m=0$ is immediate.

Inserting <a href="#rfib:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:kernel">[rfib:kernel]</a> shows that the left side of <a href="#rfib:ratio-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:ratio-kernel">[rfib:ratio-kernel]</a> is $\sum_w\int q_w\,d\nu_w$. First take the portion with $\|s-t\|\le r_*$. Uniformly in $s$, dyadic annuli and <a href="#rfib:cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:cap">[rfib:cap]</a> give <a id="rfib:near"></a>$$\label{rfib:near}
 \int_{\{\|s-t\|\le r_*\}}\|s-t\|^{-k}\,d\sigma(t)
 \le\frac{2^k r_*^{d-1-k}}
          {1-2^{-(d-1-k)}}.$$ Using $g_w\le1$, summing the at most $N$ masses $\nu_w(S^{d-1})\le2^k$, and retaining the prefactor in <a href="#rfib:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:kernel">[rfib:kernel]</a>, this portion is at most $$(2\pi)^{-k/2}\,
 \frac{N\,2^{2k} r_*^{d-1-k}}
      {1-2^{-(d-1-k)}}\le e^{Kd}.$$ Thus the state count in the near singular part is paid by the cutoff chosen in <a href="#rfib:cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:cutoff">[rfib:cutoff]</a>.

<a id="rfib:tube"></a>
For the remaining distances use the annuli $r/2<\|s-t\|\le r$ with outer radii $r=2,1,\ldots,2r_*$. Fix $w,t,r$, and push $r^{-k}\nu_w$ restricted to that annulus to the unit direction $u=(s-t)/\|s-t\|$; call the resulting measure $\mu$. It has total mass at most $2^k$ by <a href="#rfib:nu-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:nu-ball">[rfib:nu-ball]</a>. For a linear subspace $E$ of dimension $0\le j\le\ell-1$ and $0<\eta\le1$, its directions satisfy $$\label{rfib:tube}
 \mu\{u:\operatorname{dist}(u,E)\le\eta\}
 \le3^j4^k\eta^{k-j}.$$ In fact the corresponding $s$’s lie within distance $\eta r$ of the affine subspace $t+E$, with their projections there in the radius-$r$ ball about $t$. That $j$-ball has an $\eta r$-net of at most $(3/\eta)^j$ points by ambient packing. Balls of radius $2\eta r$ about these points cover the $s$’s. Applying <a href="#rfib:nu-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:nu-ball">[rfib:nu-ball]</a> and multiplying by $r^{-k}$ proves <a href="#rfib:tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:tube">[rfib:tube]</a>.

<a id="stmt-rfib-mixture"></a>
<div id="rfib:mixture" class="lemma">

<a id="rfib:mixture-dual"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Averaging perpendicular Gaussian laws). *Let $\mu$ be a finite measure on $S^{d-1}$ of mass at most $2^k$ satisfying <a href="#rfib:tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:tube">[rfib:tube]</a> for every linear subspace of dimension $j\le\ell-1$. Define the finite measure on $n\times d$ matrices $$\lambda=\int\gamma_{n,\perp u}\,d\mu(u).$$ This is a Borel measure: a perpendicular matrix can be realized as $Z(I-uu^{\mathsf T})$ with $Z\sim\gamma_n$. Then every bounded Borel function $H$ on this matrix space satisfies $$\label{rfib:mixture-dual}
 \left|\int H\,d\lambda\right|
 \le e^{Kd}\|H\|_{L^{\ell/(\ell-1)}(\gamma_n)} .$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rfib-mixture"><summary>Proof of stmt-rfib-mixture</summary>

<a id="rfib:inverse-distance"></a>
*Proof.* For $E$ of dimension $j\le\ell-1$, layer-cake integration of <a href="#rfib:tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:tube">[rfib:tube]</a> gives the inverse moment $$\label{rfib:inverse-distance}
 \int\operatorname{dist}(u,E)^{-n}\,d\mu(u)
 \le2^k+\frac{n\,3^j4^k}{k-j-n}\le e^{Kd}.$$ Here $\operatorname{dist}(u,E)\le1$; its layer-cake integral below one is $n\int_0^1\eta^{-n-1}
\mu\{\operatorname{dist}(u,E)\le\eta\}\,d\eta$. The denominator is positive because $k-j-n\ge k-\ell+1-n\ge d/10$.

<a id="rfib:inverse-gram"></a>
For $u_1,\ldots,u_\ell$, let $G=(\langle u_i,u_j\rangle)_{i,j\le\ell}$. The tube estimate, sent to $\eta\downarrow0$, shows that each proper span of at most $\ell-1$ preceding directions has $\mu$-mass zero. Thus $G$ is invertible for $\mu^{\otimes\ell}$-almost every tuple. Gram–Schmidt gives $$\det G=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}
 \operatorname{dist}\bigl(u_i,\operatorname{span}(u_1,\ldots,u_{i-1})
                    \bigr)^2 .$$ Integrate $u_\ell,u_{\ell-1},\ldots,u_1$ in that order and apply <a href="#rfib:inverse-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:inverse-distance">[rfib:inverse-distance]</a> at each step. This proves $$\label{rfib:inverse-gram}
 \int(\det G)^{-n/2}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}d\mu(u_i)
 \le e^{Kd\ell}.$$

It remains to pass from this determinant moment to singular row laws. For $0<\delta\le1$, set $$\begin{aligned}
 \alpha_\delta&=\Pr\{|Z|\le\delta\},\qquad Z\sim N(0,1),\\
 D_\delta(A)&=\int
 \frac{\mathbf 1_{\{\|Au\|_\infty\le\delta\}}}{\alpha_\delta^n}
 \,d\mu(u).
 \end{aligned}$$ There is an absolute $c>0$ such that $\alpha_\delta\ge c\delta$. Expanding the integer $\ell$-th moment under $\gamma_n$ produces one tuple $u_1,\ldots,u_\ell$. For an invertible Gram matrix $G$, the projections of each unrestricted Gaussian row onto those directions have density at most $(2\pi)^{-\ell/2}(\det G)^{-1/2}$. The box of allowed projections has volume $(2\delta)^\ell$. Row independence and <a href="#rfib:inverse-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:inverse-gram">[rfib:inverse-gram]</a> therefore give $$\mathbb E_{\gamma_n}D_\delta^\ell
 \le
 \left(\frac{(2\delta)^\ell(2\pi)^{-\ell/2}}
             {\alpha_\delta^\ell}\right)^n e^{Kd\ell}
 \le e^{Kd\ell}.$$ The null set of dependent tuples was removed above. Thus $\|D_\delta\|_{L^\ell(\gamma_n)}\le e^{Kd}$ uniformly in $\delta$.

The measure $D_\delta\,d\gamma_n$ is the mixture, with mixing measure $\mu$, of the laws conditioned on $\|Au\|_\infty\le\delta$. For each $u$, this conditioning preserves the independent perpendicular components and makes all components along $u$ tend to zero. For every bounded continuous test $H$, bounded convergence in $u$ consequently gives $\int H D_\delta\,d\gamma_n\longrightarrow\int H\,d\lambda$. Hölder’s inequality proves <a href="#rfib:mixture-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:mixture-dual">[rfib:mixture-dual]</a> for those tests. Both $\gamma_n$ and $\lambda$ are finite Euclidean Borel measures. Bounded continuous functions are dense in $L^{\ell/(\ell-1)}(\gamma_n+\lambda)$; approximation in this space passes the inequality to every bounded Borel $H$. In particular no absolute continuity of the singular mixture $\lambda$ was assumed before the dual estimate. ◻

</details>

For the far-annulus contribution to $\sum_w\int q_w\,d\nu_w$, apply Lemma <a href="#rfib:mixture" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:mixture">24</a> to the annular measure above and $H(A)=g_w(A,t)$. Since $0\le g_w\le1$, $$\int\mathbb E_{\gamma_{n,\perp u}}g_w(A,t)\,d\mu(u)
 \le e^{Kd}\bigl(\mathbb E_{\gamma_n}g_w(A,t)\bigr)^{1-1/\ell}
 =e^{Kd}f_w(t)^{1-1/\ell}.$$ On its annulus, $\|s-t\|^{-k}\le2^kr^{-k}$. The definition of $\mu$ thus bounds the annular integration in $s$ in <a href="#rfib:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:kernel">[rfib:kernel]</a> by $2^ke^{Kd}f_w(t)^{1-1/\ell}$, apart from the displayed kernel prefactor. Concavity gives, pointwise in $t$, $$\sum_w f_w(t)^{1-1/\ell}
 \le N^{1/\ell}\left(\sum_wf_w(t)\right)^{1-1/\ell}
 \le N^{1/\ell}\le e^{Kd}.$$ There are $J+1=O(d)$ far annuli. Summing them and integrating in $t$, then adding the near contribution, proves <a href="#rfib:ratio-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:ratio-kernel">[rfib:ratio-kernel]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Logarithmic combination

We can now finish Proposition <a href="#rfib:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:block">21</a>. All logarithms in the following split are integrable under the real $(S,W)$ law and the independent $C'$. Indeed $$\mathbb E[-\log f_W(S)]=H(W\mid S)\le\log N,\qquad
 \mathbb E[-\log\bar f_W(C',C'S)]=H(W\mid C',C'S)\le\log N.$$ Also <a href="#rfib:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:local-bounds">[rfib:local-bounds]</a> and $\mathbb E[-\log a_W]=H(W)\le\log N$ make $\log L_W(S)$ integrable. Finally $P_{S,W}\ll Q_{S,W}$. For any finite measure $Q'$ and probability $P'\ll Q'$, the negative part of its log density ratio has bound $$\int_{\{r<1\}}r\log(1/r)\,dQ'\le Q'(\Omega)/e,
 \qquad r=\frac{dP'}{dQ'}.$$ Applied to the marginal comparison, this and the finite upper bound <a href="#rfib:marginal-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:marginal-entropy">[rfib:marginal-entropy]</a> make $\log(f_W(S)/q_W(S))$ integrable. Hence $\log q_W(S)$ is integrable as well, and $q_W(S)>0$ almost surely under the real law.

Using the fresh $C'$ in the new-state potential, we may therefore write $$\begin{aligned}
 \Phi(W)
 &=\mathbb E\log\frac{f_W(S)}{q_W(S)}
   +\mathbb E\log\frac{q_W(S)}{L_W(S)}\\
 &\quad+\mathbb E\log\frac{L_W(S)}{\bar f_W(C',C'S)}\\
 &\le \Phi(V)+Kd
   +\log\mathbb E\frac{q_W(S)}{L_W(S)}\\
 &\quad+\log\mathbb E\frac{L_W(S)}{\bar f_W(C',C'S)}
 \le\Phi(V)+Kd .
\end{aligned}$$ The first inequality uses <a href="#rfib:marginal-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:marginal-entropy">[rfib:marginal-entropy]</a> and Jensen; the last uses <a href="#rfib:ratio-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:ratio-kernel">[rfib:ratio-kernel]</a> and Lemma <a href="#rfib:first-ratio" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:first-ratio">23</a>. This proves the proposition. The extra $k-n$ rows allowed both local comparisons to use exponent $k$, whereas only the $n$ actual rows entered the perpendicular mixture moment.

Subsection <a href="#subsec:r-fiber-application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:r-fiber-application">8.6</a> applies the estimate to a learner and combines it with stopping and terminal accuracy to obtain a sample lower bound.

<a id="sec:r-streaming"></a>
### From row comparisons to streaming lower bounds

To apply the row comparisons to the learner in Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a>, we first replace completed-measurable rules by Borel versions in the selected uniform-prior experiment. We then account for the stopping index and quantify the information that an accurate output must retain after independent auxiliary rows are revealed. These common reductions leave each comparison its own block estimate, row dimensions, and terminal residual-radius bound.

<a id="subsec:r-borel-reduction"></a>
#### Borel rules after the shared seed is fixed

Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> is applied to the original jointly measurable experiment. It supplies a fixed seed for which <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> holds and the required uniform-prior success is retained. The following reduction is performed only after that choice. It does not assert preservation of behavior at every individual signal.

<a id="stmt-lem-r-borel-kernels"></a>
<div id="lem:r-borel-kernels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Borel versions of the finite-state rules). *Fix a seed satisfying <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a>, a dimension $d\ge2$, and a deterministic finite horizon $T$. Retain the conditional law of $U_0$ at that seed. Suppose each transition and stopping probability at a positive sample index is measurable in the completion of the Borel sigma field on $\mathbb R^d\times\mathbb R$ under $$\lambda(dx,dy)=\gamma_1(dx)\,dy .$$ There are everywhere defined Borel transition and stopping kernels with the same state sets and terminal output kernels whose fixed-seed uniform-prior joint law of the signal, state path, stopping index, and output is the same as before. Their block routing probabilities are Borel functions of the entering state and the entire block data, including at states and data that have zero probability in the original run.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-r-borel-kernels"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-r-borel-kernels</summary>

*Proof.* For each state and positive index, combine the next-state and stopping choices into one finite destination set, tagging a destination as continuing or terminal. The kernel is a finite vector of completed-measurable nonnegative functions of $(x,y)$ whose sum is one. Every completed-measurable real function has a Borel version: approximate it by simple functions and replace their level sets by Borel sets modulo null sets. Choose such a version for every coordinate. The union of the exceptional sets is contained in a Borel $\lambda$-null set. On the Borel set where the chosen vector is not a probability vector, replace it by a fixed point mass. That set is also null. Since the horizon and all state sets are finite, a single Borel $\lambda$-null set $Z$ contains every sample-pair modification. A stopping choice at index zero depends only on the initial state and the fixed seed, with fresh randomness, and has no sample argument to replace.

In the fixed-seed law, <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> leaves $S\sim\sigma$ independent of the pre-generated Gaussian rows. For a nonzero row $x$, the image of $\sigma$ under $s\mapsto\langle x,s\rangle$ has a Lebesgue density when $d\ge2$. This follows from the one-coordinate sphere formula after rotation and multiplication by $\|x\|$. The zero row has Gaussian probability zero. Hence every marginal sample pair $(X_t,\langle X_t,S\rangle)$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\lambda$. In particular, $$\mathbb P\{(X_t,\langle X_t,S\rangle)\in Z\}=0.$$ For any state $u$, even one selected using previous observations, the probability that the state before time $t$ is $u$ and the current pair lies in $Z$ is bounded by this same zero probability. A finite union over the pre-generated indices therefore shows that no modified input is visited almost surely.

Couple the original and Borel finite kernels with the same fresh uniform random variable at each step. Induction makes their state paths and stopping choices identical almost surely. At identical terminal state-index pairs their output kernels agree, so the outputs may also be coupled identically. This proves the claimed joint-law equality. For any specified entering state, finite composition of the everywhere Borel kernels gives Borel block routing probabilities on every block-data vector. Such a routing function is a continuation from the specified state; it need not be a conditional law given that the state was reached. ◻

</details>

The order of these reductions matters. Original joint measurability makes the conditional success function of the shared seed measurable, so it can be averaged in Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a> then chooses versions separately for the selected seed. No jointly measurable selection of replacement versions across seed values is needed. In particular the global Borel extensions used in the finite fiber comparison are fixed before its singular comparison measure is constructed; no real-law null set is transferred to a perpendicular Gaussian law.

In each fixed-seed experiment below, unused future rows are still independent of the joint vector consisting of the signal and the current state. This follows from the pre-generated product row law, <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a>, and the fresh transition randomness in the model. If stopping is allowed at index zero, let $\overline U_0$ denote the initial padded state, including that status. It is generated from $U_0$ and fresh time-zero randomness, so $$\overline U_0\ \perp\!\!\!\perp\
       (S,(X_t)_{t\ge1})$$ in the fixed-seed law. Whenever an auxiliary matrix is introduced, it is sampled independently of the pair consisting of $S$ and the state in question. These joint independence statements justify the zero initial potentials and the fresh-block comparisons.

<a id="subsec:r-stopping-reduction"></a>
#### Common stopping and terminal estimates

The five comparisons use different information potentials. Their application to a learner shares two estimates. First we account for the stopping index before any analytic comparison is invoked. We then bound the information that success requires after auxiliary rows have been revealed.

<a id="stmt-lem-r-stopping-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:r-stopping-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Padding, output capacity, and unrestricted data). *Consider a fixed-seed Borel learner from Lemmas <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> and <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a>. Suppose its accuracy satisfies $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, its persistent states have at most $2^M$ values, and its deterministic finite horizon is $T$. Write $L=\log(1/\epsilon)$. It admits an absorbing extension, including to the end of a longer final block, with at most $$N=(T+2)2^M$$ values at each layer. The terminal output is a kernel of the extended state alone. The initial extended state is independent of the joint signal and row sequence. At every deterministic block boundary, unused future rows are independent of the pair consisting of the signal and the entering extended state. The block transition is a Borel kernel of that state and the exact block data.*

<a id="eq:r-common-output-capacity"></a>
*If its uniform-prior angular success probability is $p$, then $$\label{eq:r-common-output-capacity}
 p\le (T+1)2^M\epsilon^{d-1}.$$ For a sequence of such learners with $p\ge3/5$ and $M(d)=o(d^2)$, on the branch $T\le dL$, <a id="eq:r-common-clock"></a>$$\label{eq:r-common-clock}
 dL=O(M+1),\qquad L=o(d),\qquad
 \log N=M\log2+\log(T+2)=o(d^2).$$ All these estimates are uniform over the allowed accuracies. For all sufficiently large $d$, even a learner given all its exact observations must have $T>d/4$ to achieve success at least $3/5$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-r-stopping-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-r-stopping-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* After stopping, retain the terminal state together with its stopping index in an absorbing state. Defer the output until the end of the extension and apply its original state-index kernel. Active states and all stopped pairs number at most $(T+2)2^M$. At index zero the stopping status is generated from the data-independent initialization and fresh randomness. The independence statements then follow from the pre-generated product row law. Finite composition of the Borel rules gives the asserted block kernel, including on inputs never visited in the original run.

There are at most $(T+1)2^M$ terminal state-index labels. For each label, its output kernel has no further dependence on the signal. In the success integral for that label, bound the conditional probability of reaching it by one. Lemma <a href="#lem:r-caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-caps">3</a>, averaged over the output kernel, bounds the remaining integral by $\epsilon^{d-1}$. Summing proves <a href="#eq:r-common-output-capacity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-common-output-capacity">[eq:r-common-output-capacity]</a>, including for randomized output. When $p\ge3/5$, taking logarithms gives $$(d-1)L\le M\log2+\log(T+1)+\log(5/3).$$ For sufficiently large $d$, uniformly for $L\ge\log10$, $(d-1)L\ge3dL/4$ and $\log(dL+1)\le dL/4$. On $T\le dL$, subtraction gives $dL=O(M+1)$. Consequently $L=o(d)$, $T=o(d^2)$, and $\log N=o(d^2)$. This is a conclusion only on the stated branch; no restriction on the accuracy sequence has been imposed.

<a id="rlevels:residual-mean"></a>
For the unrestricted-data bound, suppose $T\le d/4$ and grant the output all $T$ pre-generated rows and labels, including unused pairs after a stop. Given those data, the remaining signal is uniform on a sphere of radius $R$ in a $(d-T)$-dimensional affine space. Indeed the row-space component is determined, while rotational invariance leaves a uniform direction in the kernel. More generally, if $U$ is the span of $t<d$ independent Gaussian rows, isotropy and independence give $$\label{rlevels:residual-mean}
 \mathbb E\|P_US\|^2=t/d.$$ Taking $t=T$ gives $\mathbb E(1-R^2)=T/d$, so $$\Pr\{R<1/2\}\le\frac{4T}{3d}\le\frac13.$$ On the complementary event, an output ball of radius $\epsilon$ intersecting that residual sphere is contained in a ball of radius $2\epsilon$ centered at a residual-sphere point. Rescaling by $R\ge1/2$ and applying Lemma <a href="#lem:r-caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-caps">3</a> bounds conditional success by $(4\epsilon)^{d-T-1}$. The learner’s remaining randomness has no further signal information after all data are given, so the same estimate holds after averaging its output. Thus success is at most $$\frac13+(2/5)^{3d/4-1}<\frac35$$ for large $d$, a contradiction. This argument includes $T=0$, when the residual sphere is the original unit sphere. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-r-residual-terminal"></a>
<div id="lem:r-residual-terminal" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:r-residual-terminal"></a>
<a id="eq:r-residual-markov"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Residual information in a successful state). *Let $S\sim\sigma$, let $V$ be a finite state jointly distributed with $S$, and let $G$ have $0\le k<d-1$ independent standard Gaussian rows, independently of $(S,V)$. Suppose a unit-vector output is drawn from a kernel of $V$ alone and has angular success probability at least $p$ at accuracy $\epsilon>0$. Let $R$ be the radius of the conditional sphere of $S$ given $(G,GS)$. If $$0<\rho\le1,\qquad 2\epsilon<\rho,\qquad
 \Pr\{R<\rho\}\le\eta<p,$$ then $$\label{eq:r-residual-terminal}
 I(S;V\mid G,GS)
 \ge(p-\eta)(d-k-1)\log\frac{\rho}{2\epsilon}-\log2.$$ For $\rho<1$, one available bad-radius bound is $$\label{eq:r-residual-markov}
 \Pr\{R<\rho\}\le\frac{k}{d(1-\rho^2)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-r-residual-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-r-residual-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* Let $Q$ preserve the side-data marginal $(G,GS)$ and the conditional marginals of $S,V$ given those data, while making $S$ and $V$ conditionally independent. Apply the same state-output kernel under both laws. Their relative entropy, including the output, is $I(S;V\mid G,GS)$.

The event of angular success and $R\ge\rho$ has actual probability at least $p-\eta$. Under $Q$, condition also on the output. The residual signal remains uniform on its sphere in the kernel of $G$, independently of the output. Angular success implies Euclidean error at most $\epsilon$. If the corresponding output ball intersects the residual sphere, centering it at one point of the intersection enlarges its radius to at most $2\epsilon$. Rescaling the residual sphere to unit radius and applying Lemma <a href="#lem:r-caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-caps">3</a> bounds the conditional success probability by $(2\epsilon/\rho)^{d-k-1}$.

<a id="rlevels:binary-kl"></a>
For any event with actual probability $a$ and reference probability at most $b$, where $0<b\le1$, binary relative entropy and data processing give $$\label{rlevels:binary-kl}
 D(P\Vert Q)\ge a\log(1/b)-\log2.$$ Indeed the binary entropy of $a$ is at most $\log2$. Applying this inequality to the preceding event proves <a href="#eq:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-residual-terminal">[eq:r-residual-terminal]</a>. Finally <a href="#rlevels:residual-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:residual-mean">[rlevels:residual-mean]</a> gives $\mathbb E(1-R^2)=k/d$; Markov’s inequality proves <a href="#eq:r-residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-residual-markov">[eq:r-residual-markov]</a>. ◻

</details>

We now apply the comparisons to these extended states. The critical-radius, density-level, and hybrid comparisons use an entropy bound for the outgoing state; the fiber comparison uses its number of values. The same width bound supplies both hypotheses. Each route retains its own auxiliary dimension and terminal residual-sphere estimate.

<a id="subsec:r-critical-application"></a>
#### The critical-radius comparison

<a id="stmt-thm-r-critical-streaming"></a>
<div id="thm:r-critical-streaming" class="theorem">

**Theorem 28** (Streaming consequence of the critical radius). *Let $M(d)$ be a nonnegative integer sequence with $M(d)=o(d^2)$, and let $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$. For each $d$, consider a learner in Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a> with deterministic finite horizon $T(d)$. If its uniform-sphere angular success probability, including all learner randomness, is at least $3/5$, then $$T(d)\ge c\,d\log\frac1{\epsilon(d)}$$ for an absolute $c>0$ and all sufficiently large $d$. The eventual dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-r-critical-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-r-critical-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Use Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> with $p_0=3/5$, and then Lemma <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a>. The resulting fixed-seed uniform-prior experiment retains success at least $3/5$, the conditional initial-state law, the same state bound, and the same horizon. Transition and terminal output randomness remain fresh.

Put $L=\log(1/\epsilon)\ge\log10$. The branch $T\ge dL$ already has the desired order. On $T<dL$, Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a> gives padded states of entropy at most $H_*=M\log2+\log(T+2)\le d^2$ eventually. In particular its output-capacity estimate specializes to <a id="eq:r-critical-output-capacity"></a>$$\label{eq:r-critical-output-capacity}
 \frac35\le(T+1)2^M\epsilon^{d-1}.$$ Use $B=\lceil T/m\rceil$ full padded blocks, where $m=\lfloor d/10\rfloor$ as in <a href="#eq:r-critical-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-critical-parameters">[eq:r-critical-parameters]</a>.

<a id="eq:r-critical-iteration"></a>
For a boundary state $U$, define $$J(U)=I(S;U\mid G,GS)$$ with an independent $\ell$-row matrix $G$, $\ell=\lfloor d/2\rfloor$, sampled independently of the pair $(S,U)$. In a block, write $U,W$ for the entering and exiting states and $A$ for its $m$ rows. The matrix $A$ is independent of $(S,U)$. Append an independent $r=\ell-m$ row matrix $C$ and set $G=(A;C)$. In the aligned experiment $(G,GS)$ reveals the entire exact block data $(A,AS)$. Conditional on these data and $U$, the block transition has no further dependence on $S$. Conditional data processing therefore gives $$I_1(S;W\mid G,GS)
 \le I_1(S;U\mid G,GS)=J(U).$$ The equality uses independence of $G$ from the pair $(S,U)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:r-critical-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:r-critical-comparison">10</a>, applied to the outgoing state with $H(W)\le H_*\le d^2$, yields $$J(W)\le J(U)+Kd.$$ The initial padded state $\overline U_0$ is independent of $S$, and its auxiliary matrix is independent of $(S,\overline U_0)$, so $J(\overline U_0)=0$. Iteration gives for the final padded state $W_f$ $$\label{eq:r-critical-iteration}
 J(W_f)\le Kd\left\lceil\frac{T}{m}\right\rceil .$$

<a id="eq:r-critical-radius-tail"></a>
We next lower-bound that final information. Conditional on $(G,GS)$, the row-space projection of $S$ is determined and the residual direction is uniform in $\ker G$. If $a=d-\ell$, its radius $R$ has the marginal representation $$R^2\stackrel{\mathrm{law}}=\frac{X}{X+Y},
 \qquad X\sim\chi_a^2,\quad Y\sim\chi_\ell^2$$ with independent $X,Y$. This follows by normalizing a standard Gaussian vector. Since $a\ge d/2$, the events $X\ge0.75a$ and $X+Y\le1.4d$ imply $R\ge1/2$. Chebyshev’s inequality, using variances $2a$ and $2d$, gives $$\label{eq:r-critical-radius-tail}
 \mathbb P\{R<1/2\}
 \le\frac{32}{a}+\frac{25}{2d}=O(1/d).$$

<a id="eq:r-critical-required-information"></a>
For sufficiently large $d$, the radius tail in <a href="#eq:r-critical-radius-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-critical-radius-tail">[eq:r-critical-radius-tail]</a> leaves success probability at least $1/2$ on $R\ge1/2$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-residual-terminal">27</a> with $\rho=1/2$ and that tail bound. It gives $$\label{eq:r-critical-required-information}
 J(W_f)\ge\frac{a-1}{2}\bigl(L-\log4\bigr)-\log2
          \ge c_0dL$$ for an absolute $c_0>0$ and all sufficiently large $d$. This is uniform over $L\ge\log10$, because $$L-\log4\ge
 \left(1-\frac{\log4}{\log10}\right)L>0 .$$

The common stopping reduction gives $T>d/4\ge m$, so $\lceil T/m\rceil\le2T/m$. Equation <a href="#eq:r-critical-iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-critical-iteration">[eq:r-critical-iteration]</a> is therefore an $O(T)$ upper bound. Comparing it with <a href="#eq:r-critical-required-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-critical-required-information">[eq:r-critical-required-information]</a> proves $T\ge cdL$ uniformly over the stated accuracies. ◻

</details>

<a id="rlevels:endpoints"></a>
#### The density-level comparisons

We apply both comparisons to Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a>. Assume that the original uniform-prior experiment has angular success probability at least $2/3$, that $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, and that $M=M(d)=o(d^2)$; $T$ is its deterministic finite horizon. Put $L=\log(1/\epsilon)\ge\log10$. A worst-signal success premise of $2/3$ first implies this uniform-prior premise by integration.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> to the original jointly measurable experiment with $p_0=2/3$. Fix a seed $\xi$ for which <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> holds and conditional success is at least $2/3$, retain $\mathcal L(U_0\mid\Xi=\xi)$, and then apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a>. These reductions preserve the fixed-seed uniform-prior joint law, the state bound, and the horizon. All learner statements in this subsection concern that experiment. We prove $T\ge c dL$ for an absolute $c>0$ in all sufficiently large dimensions; the eventual threshold may depend on the memory sequence.

For completeness, <a href="#rlevels:binary-kl" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:binary-kl">[rlevels:binary-kl]</a> applied to success under the product signal/output law and Lemma <a href="#lem:r-caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-caps">3</a> give the unconditional bound <a id="rlevels:output-information"></a>$$\label{rlevels:output-information}
 I(S;\widehat S)\ge\tfrac23(d-1)L-\log2.$$

<a id="rlevels:clock"></a>
Restrict to $T<dL$. Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a> provides absorbing padded states, a state-only terminal output, data-independent initial state $\overline U_0$, and fresh future blocks. Its clock bound implies, in particular, $$\label{rlevels:clock}
 L\le C\frac{M+1+\log d}{d},\qquad T=O(M+d),\qquad
 H_*:=M\log2+\log(T+2)=o(d^2).$$ The lemma also gives <a id="rlevels:linear"></a>$$\label{rlevels:linear}
 T>d/4.$$

Both comparisons below use this padded learner. Their auxiliary matrices and block lengths remain distinct.

<a id="rlevels:one-recurrence"></a>
For the one-level comparison, return to <a href="#rlevels:one-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:one-parameters">[rlevels:one-parameters]</a> and use $B_{\mathrm{one}}=\lceil T/k\rceil$ padded blocks. In a block let $Z$ and $U$ be the entering and exiting states. The next matrix $X$ is independent of the pair $(S,Z)$. Draw $B$ independently of $(S,Z,X,U)$, and put $G=(X;B)$ as in Proposition <a href="#rlevels:one-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rlevels:one-comparison">13</a>. Conditional on $(G,GS)$, the entire block data $(X,XS)$ are known. The transition kernel from $Z$ to $U$ then has no additional dependence on $S$. Conditional data processing gives $$\widetilde{\mathcal D}(U)
 \le I_{\mathrm{act}}(S;Z\mid G,GS)=\mathcal D(Z),$$ where the equality uses independence of $G$ from $(S,Z)$. By <a href="#rlevels:clock" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:clock">[rlevels:clock]</a>, $H(U)\le H_*\le d^2$ eventually, so Proposition <a href="#rlevels:one-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rlevels:one-comparison">13</a> costs at most $Cd$ per block. The initial boundary $\overline U_0$ is independent of $S$, and its auxiliary $G$ is independent of the pair $(S,\overline U_0)$; hence $\mathcal D(\overline U_0)=0$, and $$\label{rlevels:one-recurrence}
 \mathcal D(U_f)\le CdB_{\mathrm{one}}$$ for the final padded state.

<a id="rlevels:one-endpoint"></a>
Take the independent $m$-row matrix in the definition of $\mathcal D$. Its residual radius $R$ satisfies $$\Pr\{R<1/2\}\le \frac{m/d}{3/4}\le\frac13.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-residual-terminal">27</a>, with $p=2/3$, $\rho=1/2$, and $\eta=1/3$, now gives $$\label{rlevels:one-endpoint}
 \mathcal D(U_f)
 \ge\frac{d-m-1}{3}(L-\log4)-\log2
 \ge c_0dL$$ for an absolute $c_0>0$ in sufficiently large dimension. The final inequality is uniform for $L\ge\log10>\log4$. Since $T>d/4\ge k$, the common raw-data bound makes $\lceil T/k\rceil\le2T/k$. Hence <a href="#rlevels:one-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:one-recurrence">[rlevels:one-recurrence]</a> is $O(T)$, and comparison with <a href="#rlevels:one-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:one-endpoint">[rlevels:one-endpoint]</a> proves $T\ge cdL$.

<a id="rlevels:two-recurrence"></a>
For the two-label comparison, use <a href="#rlevels:two-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:two-parameters">[rlevels:two-parameters]</a> and $B_{\mathrm{two}}=\lceil T/n\rceil$ padded batches. Let $V_i$ be the state after $i$ batches. At a batch the incoming $A$ is independent of $(S,V_i)$, and $B$ is independent of the whole actual experiment. Apply Proposition <a href="#rlevels:two-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rlevels:two-comparison">14</a> to the outgoing state $V_{i+1}$; the required entropy bound follows from <a href="#rlevels:clock" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:clock">[rlevels:clock]</a> on the branch $T<dL$. Given $(D,DS)$, the exact batch $(A,AS)$ is known, so the update is conditionally a channel from $V_i$ without further access to $S$. Therefore $$\mathcal J(V_{i+1})
 \le I_{\mathrm{act}}(S;V_{i+1}\mid D,DS)+Cd
 \le I_{\mathrm{act}}(S;V_i\mid D,DS)+Cd
 =\mathcal J(V_i)+Cd.$$ The last equality uses independence of $D$ from $(S,V_i)$. Set $V_0=\overline U_0$. It is independent of $S$, and its auxiliary $G$ is independent of the pair $(S,V_0)$; hence $\mathcal J(V_0)=0$. Iteration gives $$\label{rlevels:two-recurrence}
 \mathcal J(V_{B_{\mathrm{two}}})\le CdB_{\mathrm{two}}\le C'(T+d).$$

For the independent $k$-row matrix defining $\mathcal J$, <a href="#eq:r-residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-residual-markov">[eq:r-residual-markov]</a> at $\rho=1/4$ gives $$\Pr\{R<1/4\}\le\frac{16}{15}\frac{k}{d}\le\frac8{15}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-residual-terminal">27</a> with $p=2/3$ and $\eta=8/15$. It gives <a id="rlevels:two-endpoint"></a>$$\label{rlevels:two-endpoint}
 \mathcal J(V_{B_{\mathrm{two}}})
 \ge\frac2{15}(d-k-1)(L-\log8)-\log2.$$ This is at least $cdL$ uniformly for $L\ge\log10$, since $L-\log8\ge(1-\log8/\log10)L>0$ and $d-k-1\ge d/3$. The upper bound <a href="#rlevels:two-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rlevels:two-recurrence">[rlevels:two-recurrence]</a> is $O(T)$ by $T>d/4$. This proves $T\ge cdL$; the discarded branch $T\ge dL$ already has the same order.

In both arguments the auxiliary rows are analysis variables. The learner receives only its prescribed fresh Gaussian stream with exact labels, and the comparisons permit arbitrary computation within a transition subject to the persistent finite-state restriction.

<a id="subsec:r-hybrid-application"></a>
#### The hybrid comparison: stopping and terminal information

The averaged block estimate combines with the common stopping reduction. We need a terminal information bound uniform over the auxiliary row counts in that average.

<a id="stmt-rhyb-terminal"></a>
<div id="rhyb:terminal" class="lemma">

<a id="rhyb:terminal-information"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Information in a successful terminal state). *Let $V$ be a padded final state whose unit-vector output has uniform-prior angular success at least $2/3$ at $0<\epsilon\le1/10$. For every $k_0\le k\le k_1$, $$\label{rhyb:terminal-information}
 J_k(V)\ge\frac{d-k-1}{14}\log\frac1{5\epsilon}-\log2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rhyb-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-rhyb-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* For every $k_0\le k\le k_1$, the auxiliary matrix has $k\le d/2$ rows. At $\rho=2/5$, <a href="#eq:r-residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-residual-markov">[eq:r-residual-markov]</a> therefore gives $$\Pr\{R<2/5\}\le\frac{k/d}{1-4/25}\le\frac{25}{42}.$$ The success probability is at least $2/3$, and $2/3-25/42=1/14$. Lemma <a href="#lem:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-residual-terminal">27</a> now gives <a href="#rhyb:terminal-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:terminal-information">[rhyb:terminal-information]</a>, because $\rho/(2\epsilon)=1/(5\epsilon)$. Its radius condition holds for $\epsilon\le1/10$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rhyb-conclusion"></a>
<div id="rhyb:conclusion" class="theorem">

**Theorem 30** (Precision lower bound from hybrid projections). *Let $M(d)$ be a nonnegative integer sequence with $M(d)=o(d^2)$, and let $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$. For each $d$, consider a learner in Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a> with memory bound $M(d)$, accuracy $\epsilon(d)$, deterministic horizon $T(d)\ge0$, and uniform prior on $S^{d-1}$. If $$\mathbb P\{\angle(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon(d)\}\ge\frac23,$$ then $$T(d)\ge c\,d\log\frac1{\epsilon(d)}$$ for all sufficiently large $d$. The constant $c>0$ is absolute; the dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rhyb-conclusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-rhyb-conclusion</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> with $p_0=2/3$ to the original jointly measurable experiment. Fix a seed satisfying <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> with conditional uniform-prior success at least $2/3$, retain its conditional initial-state law, and then apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a>. The fixed-seed joint experiment, state bound, and horizon are preserved.

Put $b=\log(1/\epsilon)$. In dimensions with $T>db$, the conclusion already holds. In the remaining dimensions, Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a> permits padding with $W=(T+2)2^M$ states and $B=\log W=o(d^2)$, so eventually $B\le d^2$. Break the padded computation into blocks of $h_0$ observations, with a shorter final block if necessary. The initial padded state $\overline U_0$ is independent of $S$, and the side matrix is independent of the pair $(S,\overline U_0)$. Thus $J_k(\overline U_0)=0$ for every $k$, and its averaged potential is zero. Proposition <a href="#rhyb:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:block">15</a> gives $$\overline J(V_{\rm final})
 \le Cd\left\lceil\frac{T}{h_0}\right\rceil
 \le C'(T+d)\le C''T.$$ The last inequality uses the independent bound $T>d/4$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a>; thus it also covers a shorter last block.

Lemma <a href="#rhyb:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rhyb:terminal">29</a> gives the opposite bound for every $k$ in the average. Since $k\le d/2$ and $b\ge\log10$, $$\log\frac1{5\epsilon}=b-\log5
 \ge\frac{\log2}{\log10}\,b.$$ The right side of <a href="#rhyb:terminal-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rhyb:terminal-information">[rhyb:terminal-information]</a> is consequently at least $c'db$ for all sufficiently large $d$, uniformly over the allowed accuracies. Comparing the two bounds proves $T\ge cdb$. The dimensions with $T>db$ were already covered. ◻

</details>

If a learner succeeds for every unit signal with probability at least $2/3$, it also satisfies the uniform-prior hypothesis, so the theorem applies to that worst-signal guarantee. The shared-seed case is covered by the joint-independence reduction at the start of the proof, which retains fresh randomized transition and output kernels. The proof uses exact labels and imposes no linearity or within-transition precision restriction.

<a id="subsec:r-fiber-application"></a>
#### The finite Gaussian-fiber comparison

We apply Proposition <a href="#rfib:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:block">21</a> to Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a>, including its common notation $\Xi,U_0,(X_t)_{t\ge1}$. This route also fixes the remaining randomness after choosing the seed, so its terminal count uses a deterministic decoder.

<a id="stmt-rfib-streaming"></a>
<div id="rfib:streaming" class="corollary">

**Corollary 31** (Uniform-sphere sample lower bound). *Let $M(d)\ge0$ be integer-valued with $M(d)=o(d^2)$, and let $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$. Suppose learners in Definition <a href="#def:r-streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:r-streaming-model">1</a>, with $S\sim\sigma$, satisfy $$\Pr\{\arccos\langle\widehat S,S\rangle\le\epsilon(d)\}\ge2/3,$$ where the probability includes the signal, rows, and learner randomness. Then $$T(d)\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon(d))$$ for an absolute $c>0$ and all sufficiently large $d$. The eventual dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rfib-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-rfib-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-seed-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-seed-reduction">2</a> with $p_0=2/3$ to the original jointly measurable uniform-prior experiment. Fix a seed $\xi$ satisfying <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> whose conditional success is at least $2/3$, and retain the conditional initial-state law. Then apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-borel-kernels">25</a> in that fixed-seed experiment. In particular all global Borel routing extensions are fixed before <a href="#rfib:routing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:routing">[rfib:routing]</a> and the finite measure $Q$ are formed. The two-point identity applies to those Borel functions without transferring a null set from the real observation law to a perpendicular row law.

On a product extension of this fixed-seed experiment, choose an array $\mathcal R$ of independent uniform random variables consisting of $R_t$, $0\le t\le T$, and $R^{\mathrm{out}}_{t,j}$, $0\le t\le T$, $1\le j\le2^M$. Choose the whole array independently of $(U_0,S,(X_t)_{t\ge1})$. The factorization <a href="#eq:r-seed-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-seed-factorization">[eq:r-seed-factorization]</a> then implies that the complete conditional choice vector $(U_0,\mathcal R)$ is independent of $(S,(X_t)_{t\ge1})$.

Enumerate each finite state alphabet by slots $j\le2^M$. Use $R_0$ for a stopping choice before the first sample and, at time $t\ge1$, use $R_t$ to realize the joint next-state and stopping outcome by the inverse distribution function of its finite probability row. Use $R^{\mathrm{out}}_{t,j}$ to realize the unit-vector output kernel at terminal pair $(t,j)$. Such a realization can be chosen Borel for a probability kernel on the standard Borel sphere, by mapping the sphere into a Borel subset of $[0,1]$ and using an inverse distribution function. The resulting fixed-seed experiment is jointly measurable and has the same conditional law as the Borel learner.

Its success probability averaged over $(U_0,\mathcal R)$ is at least $2/3$. Fubini and averaging give a complete realization with uniform-prior success at least $3/5$. Fix it. The initial state, all transition and stopping rules, and all terminal outputs are now deterministic. Independence of the complete choice vector leaves the joint law of the signal and all rows unchanged. Thus the complete vector $(\xi,U_0,\mathcal R)$, including the seed and initialization, is fixed before any state comparison. Its unused entries add no persistent learner state.

Put $X_*=d\log(1/\epsilon)$, and restrict to $T<X_*$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a> to the fixed decoder with success at least $3/5$. Its output count gives <a id="rfib:output-count"></a>$$\label{rfib:output-count}
 (d-1)\log(1/\epsilon)
 \le M\log2+\log(T+1)+\log(5/3).$$ The common short-run estimate yields $X_*=O(M+1)$, and the absorbing padded state has width at most <a id="rfib:width"></a>$$\label{rfib:width}
 \begin{aligned}
 N&=(T+2)2^M,\\
 \log N&\le M\log2+\log(X_*+2)=O(M+1)\le d^2
 \end{aligned}$$ for all sufficiently large $d$. The padded decoder is a function of this state, and rows generated after stopping remain fresh.

<a id="rfib:iteration"></a>
<a id="rfib:terminal"></a>
Pad also to $\lceil T/n\rceil$ full $n$-row blocks. Each block’s row matrix is independent of the pair consisting of the signal and old state, while its labels remain exact. After the complete fixation, the initial padded state is deterministic, so its potential is zero. Iterating Proposition <a href="#rfib:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:block">21</a> for the padded final state $F$ gives $$\label{rfib:iteration}
 \Phi(F)\le Kd\lceil T/n\rceil.$$ For a lower bound use the independent $k$-row matrix in $\Phi(F)$. Equation <a href="#eq:r-residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:r-residual-markov">[eq:r-residual-markov]</a> gives $$\Pr\{R<1/2\}\le\frac{4k}{3d}\le\frac49.$$ The fixed decoder has success at least $3/5$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:r-residual-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-residual-terminal">27</a> with $p=3/5$, $\rho=1/2$, and $\eta=4/9$, and write $p_0=3/5-4/9>0$. It gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \Phi(F)
 &\ge p_0(d-k-1)\log(1/(4\epsilon))-\log2 \notag\\
 &\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon)                              \label{rfib:terminal}
\end{aligned}$$ for sufficiently large $d$. The constant is absolute because $d-k-1\ge d/2$ and $\log(1/(4\epsilon))\ge
 (1-\log4/\log10)\log(1/\epsilon)$ on the stated accuracy range.

Finally, Lemma <a href="#lem:r-stopping-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:r-stopping-reduction">26</a> gives $T>d/4\ge n$, including the exclusion of $T=0$. Hence $\lceil T/n\rceil\le2T/n$, and $d/n$ is absolutely bounded for large $d$. The upper bound <a href="#rfib:iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:iteration">[rfib:iteration]</a> is therefore $O(T)$. Combining it with <a href="#rfib:terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rfib:terminal">[rfib:terminal]</a> proves the corollary. ◻

</details>

If a learner has angular success at least $2/3$ for every fixed signal, averaging that guarantee over the uniform sphere supplies the hypothesis of Corollary <a href="#rfib:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rfib:streaming">31</a>. It therefore gives the same lower bound with the stated worst-signal premise.
